I wish I could say I’m just choosing to have extra, but once it starts, it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control.\n\nIt’s like that switch I mentioned… I’ll be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, just stop, and then I’m still opening another container or going back for more. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m watching myself do it instead of being able to decide.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or purging?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in a planned way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I might tell myself I’ll “be better tomorrow” or maybe I should move more, but I don’t… purge or anything like that. I mostly just feel bad and try to sort of move on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: I guess… more often than I’d like to admit.\n\nI started looking back through my journal because I didn’t want to exaggerate, and there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I try not to let it affect things too much, but it does.

After it happens I get really ashamed and kind of stuck in my head about it. I’ve stopped inviting people over as much because I don’t want anyone noticing anything, or I just don’t feel like I can face people. And some evenings I’ll skip things I normally like or need to do, like yoga or even gardening chores, because I’m too preoccupied with how bad I feel.

I don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely gotten in the way.

But honestly, not really. Once I start, it’s like this awful switch flips. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop now, and my hands are still opening containers like I’m not the one in charge.\n\nThat sounds dramatic, doesn’t it? But that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating that much in one sitting?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean, I feel bad about it, yes, but it’s more like I’m disappointed in myself, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Why did I do that again?”  \n\nI know people overeat sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is… but afterward I’m usually not feeling very good about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever eaten an amount of food that's definitely larger than most people would eat in a similar time period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, but when I look at it honestly, yes.\n\nLike, I’ll buy a family-size takeout order and then still stop for chips or something from the bakery, and somehow I end up eating most of it myself in one evening. I wish I could say I’m just choosing to have extra, but once it starts, it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control.\n\nIt’s like that switch I mentioned… I’ll be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, just stop, and then I’m still opening another container or going back for more. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m watching myself do it instead of being able to decide.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or purging?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Like I’ll think, “Why did I do that again?”  \n\nI know people overeat sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is… but afterward I’m usually not feeling very good about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever eaten an amount of food that's definitely larger than most people would eat in a similar time period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, but when I look at it honestly, yes.\n\nLike, I’ll buy a family-size takeout order and then still stop for chips or something from the bakery, and somehow I end up eating most of it myself in one evening. At the time I don’t always register how much it is, but afterward it’s pretty clear that it’s more than most people would eat in that amount of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish I could say I’m just choosing to have extra, but once it starts, it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control.\n\nIt’s like that switch I mentioned… I’ll be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, just stop, and then I’m still opening another container or going back for more. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m watching myself do it instead of being able to decide.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or purging?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in a planned way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I might tell myself I’ll “be better tomorrow” or maybe I should move more, but I don’t… purge or anything like that. And I don’t think I’ve really exercised to make up for it, not like that. I mostly just feel bad and try to sort of move on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: I guess… more often than I’d like to admit.\n\nI started looking back through my journal because I didn’t want to exaggerate, and there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. Most weeks, it’s… several times. I know that probably sounds like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating and the feelings that follow them cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to let it affect things too much, but it does.\n\nAfter it happens I get really ashamed and kind of stuck in my head about it. I’ve stopped inviting people over as much because I don’t want anyone noticing anything, or I just don’t feel like I can face people. And some evenings I’ll skip things I normally like or need to do, like yoga or even gardening chores, because I’m too preoccupied with how bad I feel.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely gotten in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I’ll be thinking, Taylor, put it away, just stop now, and then it’s like this awful switch flips. My hands are still opening containers or getting more food, and I feel like I’m not really the one in charge in that moment.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t weigh myself very consistently, to be honest… partly because I don’t really like looking at the number.\n\nMy clothes feel maybe a little different some weeks, but I don’t know if I’d call it a significant change. I’m sorry, I know that’s not very precise.\n\nDoctor: Does this loss of control when eating usually happen in the evenings or can it happen at other times of the day as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly that same feeling whenever it happens, not so much the exact time, I think.\n\nI guess evenings are common, but the thing I notice is once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips. I can be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, stop, and my hands are still opening containers or getting more, like I’m not the one in charge.\n\nSorry, I don’t know if that answers it the way you meant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I’ll think, “Why did I do that again?”  \n\nI know people overeat sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is… but afterward I’m usually not feeling very good about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever eaten an amount of food that's definitely larger than most people would eat in a similar time period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, but when I look at it honestly, yes.\n\nLike, I’ll buy a family-size takeout order and then still stop for chips or something from the bakery, and somehow I end up eating most of it myself in one evening. At the time I don’t always register how much it is, but afterward it’s pretty clear that it’s more than most people would eat in that amount of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish I could say I’m just choosing to have extra, but once it starts, it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control.\n\nIt’s like that switch I mentioned… I’ll be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, just stop, and then I’m still opening another container or going back for more. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m watching myself do it instead of being able to decide.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or purging?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in a planned way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I might tell myself I’ll “be better tomorrow” or maybe I should move more, but I don’t… purge or anything like that. And I don’t think I’ve really exercised to make up for it, not like that. I mostly just feel bad and try to sort of move on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: I guess… more often than I’d like to admit.\n\nI started looking back through my journal because I didn’t want to exaggerate, and there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. Most weeks, it’s… several times. I know that probably sounds like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating and the feelings that follow them cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to let it affect things too much, but it does.\n\nAfter it happens I get really ashamed and kind of stuck in my head about it. I’ve stopped inviting people over as much because I don’t want anyone noticing anything, or I just don’t feel like I can face people. And some evenings I’ll skip things I normally like or need to do, like yoga or even gardening chores, because I’m too preoccupied with how bad I feel.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely gotten in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I’ll be thinking, Taylor, put it away, just stop now, and then it’s like this awful switch flips. My hands are still opening containers or getting more food, and I feel like I’m not really the one in charge in that moment.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t weigh myself very consistently, to be honest… partly because I don’t really like looking at the number.\n\nMy clothes feel maybe a little different some weeks, but I don’t know if I’d call it a significant change. I’m sorry, I know that’s not very precise.\n\nDoctor: Does this loss of control when eating usually happen in the evenings or can it happen at other times of the day as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly that same feeling whenever it happens, not so much the exact time, I think.\n\nI guess evenings are common, but the thing I notice is once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips. I can be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, stop, and my hands are still opening containers or getting more, like I’m not the one in charge.\n\nSorry, I don’t know if that answers it the way you meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI mean, I don’t feel good afterward, if that’s what you mean. I usually try not to think about it too much because then I feel embarrassed, I guess. But “guilty” feels like a big word… maybe sometimes, yes. I don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of losing control while eating ever happen to you during the day, outside of the evenings?\n\nPatient: Yes… it can happen during the day too. I think I made it sound like it’s only evenings, but that’s not completely true.\n\nIt’s more like, once I start, there’s this switch that flips. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, you don’t need more, and then I’m still reaching for things or opening another container, like I’m not really the one in charge. I’m sorry, that sounds dramatic, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mostly just feel bad and try to sort of move on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: I guess… more often than I’d like to admit.\n\nI started looking back through my journal because I didn’t want to exaggerate, and there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. Most weeks, it’s… several times. I know that probably sounds like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating and the feelings that follow them cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to let it affect things too much, but it does.\n\nAfter it happens I get really ashamed and kind of stuck in my head about it. I’ve stopped inviting people over as much because I don’t want anyone noticing anything, or I just don’t feel like I can face people. And some evenings I’ll skip things I normally like or need to do, like yoga or even gardening chores, because I’m too preoccupied with how bad I feel.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely gotten in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I’ll be thinking, Taylor, put it away, just stop now, and then it’s like this awful switch flips. My hands are still opening containers or getting more food, and I feel like I’m not really the one in charge in that moment.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t weigh myself very consistently, to be honest… partly because I don’t really like looking at the number.\n\nMy clothes feel maybe a little different some weeks, but I don’t know if I’d call it a significant change. I’m sorry, I know that’s not very precise.\n\nDoctor: Does this loss of control when eating usually happen in the evenings or can it happen at other times of the day as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly that same feeling whenever it happens, not so much the exact time, I think.\n\nI guess evenings are common, but the thing I notice is once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips. I can be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, stop, and my hands are still opening containers or getting more, like I’m not the one in charge.\n\nSorry, I don’t know if that answers it the way you meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI mean, I don’t feel good afterward, if that’s what you mean. I usually try not to think about it too much because then I feel embarrassed, I guess. But “guilty” feels like a big word… maybe sometimes, yes. I don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of losing control while eating ever happen to you during the day, outside of the evenings?\n\nPatient: Yes… it can happen during the day too. I think I made it sound like it’s only evenings, but that’s not completely true.\n\nIt’s more like, once I start, there’s this switch that flips. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, you don’t need more, and then I’m still reaching for things or opening another container, like I’m not really the one in charge. I’m sorry, that sounds dramatic, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty while you're eating and feel like you can't stop, or is the guilt something that comes after you've finished eating?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe more after. While it’s happening, I’m mostly caught up in trying to stop and not really stopping, if that makes sense.\n\nThere might be some embarrassment even during it, like, why am I doing this, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt right then. Afterward is when I feel worse about it. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not trying to be dramatic—I just… I’m having a hard time getting a handle on it.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating that much in the evenings, do you feel like you can stop if you want to?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, because that sounds… better, I guess. I’ve stopped inviting people over as much because I don’t want anyone noticing anything, or I just don’t feel like I can face people. And some evenings I’ll skip things I normally like or need to do, like yoga or even gardening chores, because I’m too preoccupied with how bad I feel.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely gotten in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I’ll be thinking, Taylor, put it away, just stop now, and then it’s like this awful switch flips. My hands are still opening containers or getting more food, and I feel like I’m not really the one in charge in that moment.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t weigh myself very consistently, to be honest… partly because I don’t really like looking at the number.\n\nMy clothes feel maybe a little different some weeks, but I don’t know if I’d call it a significant change. I’m sorry, I know that’s not very precise.\n\nDoctor: Does this loss of control when eating usually happen in the evenings or can it happen at other times of the day as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly that same feeling whenever it happens, not so much the exact time, I think.\n\nI guess evenings are common, but the thing I notice is once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips. I can be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, stop, and my hands are still opening containers or getting more, like I’m not the one in charge.\n\nSorry, I don’t know if that answers it the way you meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI mean, I don’t feel good afterward, if that’s what you mean. I usually try not to think about it too much because then I feel embarrassed, I guess. But “guilty” feels like a big word… maybe sometimes, yes. I don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of losing control while eating ever happen to you during the day, outside of the evenings?\n\nPatient: Yes… it can happen during the day too. I think I made it sound like it’s only evenings, but that’s not completely true.\n\nIt’s more like, once I start, there’s this switch that flips. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, you don’t need more, and then I’m still reaching for things or opening another container, like I’m not really the one in charge. I’m sorry, that sounds dramatic, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty while you're eating and feel like you can't stop, or is the guilt something that comes after you've finished eating?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe more after. While it’s happening, I’m mostly caught up in trying to stop and not really stopping, if that makes sense.\n\nThere might be some embarrassment even during it, like, why am I doing this, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt right then. Afterward is when I feel worse about it. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel self-criticism or judgment towards yourself while you're eating and losing control, or is that something that happens afterward?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I separate those very well.\n\nWhile I’m eating, I think there is some of that, like a voice saying, why can’t you just stop, what are you doing. But I’m also sort of trying to push it away because if I think about it too much, I feel worse. Afterward is probably when I judge myself more clearly, I guess.\n\nI’m sorry, I’m not trying to be vague. It’s just hard to say it neatly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mostly just feel bad and try to sort of move on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: I guess… more often than I’d like to admit.\n\nI started looking back through my journal because I didn’t want to exaggerate, and there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. Most weeks, it’s… several times. I know that probably sounds like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating and the feelings that follow them cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to let it affect things too much, but it does.\n\nAfter it happens I get really ashamed and kind of stuck in my head about it. I’ve stopped inviting people over as much because I don’t want anyone noticing anything, or I just don’t feel like I can face people. And some evenings I’ll skip things I normally like or need to do, like yoga or even gardening chores, because I’m too preoccupied with how bad I feel.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely gotten in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I’ll be thinking, Taylor, put it away, just stop now, and then it’s like this awful switch flips. My hands are still opening containers or getting more food, and I feel like I’m not really the one in charge in that moment.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t weigh myself very consistently, to be honest… partly because I don’t really like looking at the number.\n\nMy clothes feel maybe a little different some weeks, but I don’t know if I’d call it a significant change. I’m sorry, I know that’s not very precise.\n\nDoctor: Does this loss of control when eating usually happen in the evenings or can it happen at other times of the day as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly that same feeling whenever it happens, not so much the exact time, I think.\n\nI guess evenings are common, but the thing I notice is once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips. I can be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, stop, and my hands are still opening containers or getting more, like I’m not the one in charge.\n\nSorry, I don’t know if that answers it the way you meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI mean, I don’t feel good afterward, if that’s what you mean. I usually try not to think about it too much because then I feel embarrassed, I guess. But “guilty” feels like a big word… maybe sometimes, yes. I don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of losing control while eating ever happen to you during the day, outside of the evenings?\n\nPatient: Yes… it can happen during the day too. I think I made it sound like it’s only evenings, but that’s not completely true.\n\nIt’s more like, once I start, there’s this switch that flips. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, you don’t need more, and then I’m still reaching for things or opening another container, like I’m not really the one in charge. I’m sorry, that sounds dramatic, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty while you're eating and feel like you can't stop, or is the guilt something that comes after you've finished eating?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe more after. While it’s happening, I’m mostly caught up in trying to stop and not really stopping, if that makes sense.\n\nThere might be some embarrassment even during it, like, why am I doing this, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt right then. Afterward is when I feel worse about it. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel self-criticism or judgment towards yourself while you're eating and losing control, or is that something that happens afterward?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I separate those very well.\n\nWhile I’m eating, I think there is some of that, like a voice saying, why can’t you just stop, what are you doing. But I’m also sort of trying to push it away because if I think about it too much, I feel worse. Afterward is probably when I judge myself more clearly, I guess.\n\nI’m sorry, I’m not trying to be vague. It’s just hard to say it neatly.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating and feel like you've lost control, are you eating faster than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single bite, but during those times, yes, I eat much faster than I normally would.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but sometimes I’m just standing there at the dining room table, with the microwave stuff and boxes around me, and I’m swallowing one bite while I’m already reaching for the next. I barely even taste it. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about, but… yes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, but when I look at it honestly, yes.\n\nLike, I’ll buy a family-size takeout order and then still stop for chips or something from the bakery, and somehow I end up eating most of it myself in one evening. At the time I don’t always register how much it is, but afterward it’s pretty clear that it’s more than most people would eat in that amount of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish I could say I’m just choosing to have extra, but once it starts, it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control.\n\nIt’s like that switch I mentioned… I’ll be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, just stop, and then I’m still opening another container or going back for more. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m watching myself do it instead of being able to decide.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or purging?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in a planned way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I might tell myself I’ll “be better tomorrow” or maybe I should move more, but I don’t… purge or anything like that. And I don’t think I’ve really exercised to make up for it, not like that. I mostly just feel bad and try to sort of move on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: I guess… more often than I’d like to admit.\n\nI started looking back through my journal because I didn’t want to exaggerate, and there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. Most weeks, it’s… several times. I know that probably sounds like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating and the feelings that follow them cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to let it affect things too much, but it does.\n\nAfter it happens I get really ashamed and kind of stuck in my head about it. I’ve stopped inviting people over as much because I don’t want anyone noticing anything, or I just don’t feel like I can face people. And some evenings I’ll skip things I normally like or need to do, like yoga or even gardening chores, because I’m too preoccupied with how bad I feel.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely gotten in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I’ll be thinking, Taylor, put it away, just stop now, and then it’s like this awful switch flips. My hands are still opening containers or getting more food, and I feel like I’m not really the one in charge in that moment.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t weigh myself very consistently, to be honest… partly because I don’t really like looking at the number.\n\nMy clothes feel maybe a little different some weeks, but I don’t know if I’d call it a significant change. I’m sorry, I know that’s not very precise.\n\nDoctor: Does this loss of control when eating usually happen in the evenings or can it happen at other times of the day as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly that same feeling whenever it happens, not so much the exact time, I think.\n\nI guess evenings are common, but the thing I notice is once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips. I can be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, stop, and my hands are still opening containers or getting more, like I’m not the one in charge.\n\nSorry, I don’t know if that answers it the way you meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI mean, I don’t feel good afterward, if that’s what you mean. I usually try not to think about it too much because then I feel embarrassed, I guess. But “guilty” feels like a big word… maybe sometimes, yes. I don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of losing control while eating ever happen to you during the day, outside of the evenings?\n\nPatient: Yes… it can happen during the day too. I think I made it sound like it’s only evenings, but that’s not completely true.\n\nIt’s more like, once I start, there’s this switch that flips. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, you don’t need more, and then I’m still reaching for things or opening another container, like I’m not really the one in charge. I’m sorry, that sounds dramatic, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty while you're eating and feel like you can't stop, or is the guilt something that comes after you've finished eating?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe more after. While it’s happening, I’m mostly caught up in trying to stop and not really stopping, if that makes sense.\n\nThere might be some embarrassment even during it, like, why am I doing this, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt right then. Afterward is when I feel worse about it. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel self-criticism or judgment towards yourself while you're eating and losing control, or is that something that happens afterward?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I separate those very well.\n\nWhile I’m eating, I think there is some of that, like a voice saying, why can’t you just stop, what are you doing. But I’m also sort of trying to push it away because if I think about it too much, I feel worse. Afterward is probably when I judge myself more clearly, I guess.\n\nI’m sorry, I’m not trying to be vague. It’s just hard to say it neatly.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating and feel like you've lost control, are you eating faster than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single bite, but during those times, yes, I eat much faster than I normally would.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but sometimes I’m just standing there at the dining room table, with the microwave stuff and boxes around me, and I’m swallowing one bite while I’m already reaching for the next. I barely even taste it. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about, but… yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually, I think.\n\nI don’t mean to make it sound dramatic, but I do keep going past where I should stop. By the end my stomach feels really tight, sometimes painful, and I’ll just sit very still on the couch afterward because moving around feels bad. Even walking upstairs can feel uncomfortable.\n\nThat’s probably the kind of “uncomfortably full” you mean, right?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I can be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, stop, and my hands are still opening containers or getting more, like I’m not the one in charge.\n\nSorry, I don’t know if that answers it the way you meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI mean, I don’t feel good afterward, if that’s what you mean. But that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating that much in one sitting?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean, I feel bad about it, yes, but it’s more like I’m disappointed in myself, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Why did I do that again?”  \n\nI know people overeat sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is… but afterward I’m usually not feeling very good about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever eaten an amount of food that's definitely larger than most people would eat in a similar time period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, but when I look at it honestly, yes.\n\nLike, I’ll buy a family-size takeout order and then still stop for chips or something from the bakery, and somehow I end up eating most of it myself in one evening. At the time I don’t always register how much it is, but afterward it’s pretty clear that it’s more than most people would eat in that amount of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish I could say I’m just choosing to have extra, but once it starts, it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control.\n\nIt’s like that switch I mentioned… I’ll be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, just stop, and then I’m still opening another container or going back for more. I know that probably sounds strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m watching myself do it instead of being able to decide.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or purging?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in a planned way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I might tell myself I’ll “be better tomorrow” or maybe I should move more, but I don’t… purge or anything like that. And I don’t think I’ve really exercised to make up for it, not like that. I mostly just feel bad and try to sort of move on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: I guess… more often than I’d like to admit.\n\nI started looking back through my journal because I didn’t want to exaggerate, and there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. Most weeks, it’s… several times. I know that probably sounds like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating and the feelings that follow them cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to let it affect things too much, but it does.\n\nAfter it happens I get really ashamed and kind of stuck in my head about it. I’ve stopped inviting people over as much because I don’t want anyone noticing anything, or I just don’t feel like I can face people. And some evenings I’ll skip things I normally like or need to do, like yoga or even gardening chores, because I’m too preoccupied with how bad I feel.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely gotten in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I’ll be thinking, Taylor, put it away, just stop now, and then it’s like this awful switch flips. My hands are still opening containers or getting more food, and I feel like I’m not really the one in charge in that moment.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t weigh myself very consistently, to be honest… partly because I don’t really like looking at the number.\n\nMy clothes feel maybe a little different some weeks, but I don’t know if I’d call it a significant change. I’m sorry, I know that’s not very precise.\n\nDoctor: Does this loss of control when eating usually happen in the evenings or can it happen at other times of the day as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly that same feeling whenever it happens, not so much the exact time, I think.\n\nI guess evenings are common, but the thing I notice is once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips. I can be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, stop, and my hands are still opening containers or getting more, like I’m not the one in charge.\n\nSorry, I don’t know if that answers it the way you meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI mean, I don’t feel good afterward, if that’s what you mean. I usually try not to think about it too much because then I feel embarrassed, I guess. But “guilty” feels like a big word… maybe sometimes, yes. I don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of losing control while eating ever happen to you during the day, outside of the evenings?\n\nPatient: Yes… it can happen during the day too. I think I made it sound like it’s only evenings, but that’s not completely true.\n\nIt’s more like, once I start, there’s this switch that flips. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, you don’t need more, and then I’m still reaching for things or opening another container, like I’m not really the one in charge. I’m sorry, that sounds dramatic, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty while you're eating and feel like you can't stop, or is the guilt something that comes after you've finished eating?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe more after. While it’s happening, I’m mostly caught up in trying to stop and not really stopping, if that makes sense.\n\nThere might be some embarrassment even during it, like, why am I doing this, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt right then. Afterward is when I feel worse about it. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel self-criticism or judgment towards yourself while you're eating and losing control, or is that something that happens afterward?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I separate those very well.\n\nWhile I’m eating, I think there is some of that, like a voice saying, why can’t you just stop, what are you doing. But I’m also sort of trying to push it away because if I think about it too much, I feel worse. Afterward is probably when I judge myself more clearly, I guess.\n\nI’m sorry, I’m not trying to be vague. It’s just hard to say it neatly.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating and feel like you've lost control, are you eating faster than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single bite, but during those times, yes, I eat much faster than I normally would.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but sometimes I’m just standing there at the dining room table, with the microwave stuff and boxes around me, and I’m swallowing one bite while I’m already reaching for the next. I barely even taste it. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about, but… yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually, I think.\n\nI don’t mean to make it sound dramatic, but I do keep going past where I should stop. By the end my stomach feels really tight, sometimes painful, and I’ll just sit very still on the couch afterward because moving around feels bad. Even walking upstairs can feel uncomfortable.\n\nThat’s probably the kind of “uncomfortably full” you mean, right?\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of losing control while eating only happen when you're having one of these large episodes, or can it also happen when you're just eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly with the bigger episodes, I think. I don’t usually feel that way with a normal meal… or at least not in the same way.\n\nWhen it happens, it’s like once I start, this awful switch flips. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, just stop now, and my hands are still opening containers or getting more food like I’m not really the one in charge. That sounds strange when I say it out loud, but that’s the closest I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do these episodes of eating a large amount of food typically happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I wish it were less than it is. I’ve been trying to keep a journal, and when I look back, there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once.\n\nMost weeks… it’s several times. I know that probably sounds like a lot. I’m not sure if I’m counting it the right way, but that’s what it looks like when I’m honest about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of losing control while eating only happen when you're eating a very large amount of food, or can it also happen when you're eating a normal amount?\n\nPatient: I think it’s really the large amount episodes, mostly. I don’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nIt’s that once I start, there’s this awful… switch, I guess, that flips. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop now, and my hands just keep opening containers or getting more like I’m not fully the one in charge. With a normal meal, I don’t usually feel that same loss of control.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F50.8"], "note": "F50.4 was ruled out as the patient does not exhibit behaviors of purging or excessive exercise to compensate for the binge eating episodes, which is more characteristic of bulimia nervosa. The patient's description of feeling a loss of control during episodes of eating large amounts of food, feeling embarrassed and ashamed afterward, and the frequency of these episodes support the diagnosis of F50.8, other eating disorders, specifically binge eating disorder.", "is_final": true}
